are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: On a few of the most wired days, I could hear a low humming voice like wind over rock telling me where the dancers should move, even when I was alone in my apartment with the windows closed.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: When I was buzzing with ideas I’d skip meals without noticing, and now food tastes like cardboard, so my rehearsal clothes have been hanging looser than usual.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: My sleep has been all over the place—some nights I stayed up sketching formations until dawn, and lately I fall asleep exhausted but wake at four or five with my stomach tight.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: Recently even crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels slowed down, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of music.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: I feel wiped out after small things, like answering one email to a dancer or showering with this ankle brace on, and lying down doesn’t really recharge me.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying the cancelled performances and telling myself I let the dancers, the funders, and the whole community down, even though people have been kinder about it than I deserve.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: There were stretches when I felt dazzlingly alive and then suddenly furious if anyone questioned the plan or reminded me my ankle still needed rest.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: Even with the injury, I couldn’t stop moving from task to task—drawing maps, emailing collaborators, reorganizing music files, and trying to redesign the whole project in one rush.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I honestly believed I could turn this setback into a career-defining work that would change how people think about dance and wilderness, and I was shocked when others didn’t immediately see it.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My collaborators told me I was talking so fast in meetings that they couldn’t get a word in, and I remember feeling like if I paused the whole idea would disappear.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I’d sit down to finish a grant presenters, dancers I barely knew, and old professors with long proposals, as if I had to build an entire new network for the piece right that minute.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in dancing or other things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I still care about dancing, and I don’t want to sound like I’ve just given up or anything. It’s just… some days it feels harder to feel the same spark. But I don’t know if that’s “lost interest,” exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a lot, anyway. Is that what you mean by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel pleasurable right now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I wouldn’t say things feel completely empty or anything. Maybe some things don’t land the same way right now, but I can’t tell if that’s just being tired or distracted. I still want to enjoy them, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, not in a terrible way, but I’ve noticed it. Like I’ll sit down to finish something straightforward, like a grant report, and then a truck outside or a photo on the wall or even one phrase in an old rehearsal note will pull me into some totally different idea or plan. But yes, it does feel… bigger than that, I think. I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like everything’s technically there—the lights, the room—but there’s no music in me. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everyone does, I guess—but this waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness… like everything’s there but there’s no music in me… that feels different. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in dancing or other things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I still care about dancing, and I don’t want to sound like I’ve just given up or anything. It’s just… some days it feels harder to feel the same spark. But I don’t know if that’s “lost interest,” exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a lot, anyway. I mean, I get worried here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It feels more like I’m just unsettled sometimes. Is that what you mean by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel pleasurable right now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I wouldn’t say things feel completely empty or anything. Maybe some things don’t land the same way right now, but I can’t tell if that’s just being tired or distracted. I still want to enjoy them, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, not in a terrible way, but I’ve noticed it. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you been waking up earlier than usual and not able to fall back asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every single night, but lately I’ll fall asleep because I’m just exhausted, and then I’m up around four or five with my stomach all tight, and I can’t really settle back down.\n\nBefore that there were a few nights where I stayed up way too late sketching formations, almost until dawn, but I told myself that was just work energy. I don’t know if that counts as sleep being “disturbed,” but it’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you were buzzing with ideas, did you feel unusually energized or high compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “unusually energized” is fair. I’d feel furious suddenly, like they were trying to slow down something important. It’s been about two months now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Does the sadness feel much stronger than you would expect from being injured?\n\nPatient: I mean… I keep telling myself it’s just an ankle, and people deal with worse, so maybe I shouldn’t make too much of it. But yes, it does feel… bigger than that, I think. I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like everything’s technically there—the lights, the room—but there’s no music in me. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everyone does, I guess—but this waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness… like everything’s there but there’s no music in me… that feels different. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in dancing or other things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I still care about dancing, and I don’t want to sound like I’ve just given up or anything. It’s just… some days it feels harder to feel the same spark. But I don’t know if that’s “lost interest,” exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a lot, anyway. I mean, I get worried here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It feels more like I’m just unsettled sometimes. Is that what you mean by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel pleasurable right now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I wouldn’t say things feel completely empty or anything. Maybe some things don’t land the same way right now, but I can’t tell if that’s just being tired or distracted. I still want to enjoy them, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, not in a terrible way, but I’ve noticed it. Like I’ll sit down to finish something straightforward, like a grant report, and then a truck outside or a photo on the wall or even one phrase in an old rehearsal note will pull me into some totally different idea or plan. I can usually come back to it eventually, but it’s been harder to stay on one thing. Is that what you mean by concentrating?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has gone down lately?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but when I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just forget to eat sometimes, like I’d look up and realize I’d skipped a meal. And now… food is there, but it kind of tastes like cardboard. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging a bit looser, so I guess my appetite has gone down. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you been waking up earlier than usual and not able to fall back asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every single night, but lately I’ll fall asleep because I’m just exhausted, and then I’m up around four or five with my stomach all tight, and I can’t really settle back down.\n\nBefore that there were a few nights where I stayed up way too late sketching formations, almost until dawn, but I told myself that was just work energy. I don’t know if that counts as sleep being “disturbed,” but it’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you were buzzing with ideas, did you feel unusually energized or high compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “unusually energized” is fair. I don’t want to overstate it, but there were stretches where I felt kind of dazzlingly alive, like every idea had light around it and I could see the whole piece coming together.\n\nAnd then, um… not as flattering, but I’d get really sharp with people if they questioned the plan or reminded me my ankle still needed rest. I’d feel furious suddenly, like they were trying to slow down something important. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did you feel unusually confident or powerful compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that way. At the time it didn’t feel like “powerful,” exactly. It felt more like I could turn the whole setback into something meaningful—like a career-defining piece, something that might genuinely change how people think about dance and wilderness.\n\nAnd I remember being kind of… shocked, honestly, when other people didn’t immediately see what I saw. I thought it was so obvious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, were you taking on a lot more projects or working much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than usual. I mean, I told myself it was just being productive, trying to make good use of being injured, but I was kind of bouncing from one thing to the next.\n\nI’d be drawing maps, then emailing collaborators, then reorganizing music files, then suddenly trying to redesign the whole project all at once. It felt important at the time, not chaotic exactly… but looking back, it was a lot. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did they cause serious problems in your life or work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I was still showing up for the essential things, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything completely fell apart.\n\nBut, yes, it did cause problems. I fell behind on my teaching prep, and I missed a grant deadline, which is… not like me. And the studio got so disorganized that collaborators started taking over basic scheduling things for me.\n\nI could still function, technically, but not at the level I normally do as a choreographer or teacher. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But yes, it does feel… bigger than that, I think. I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like everything’s technically there—the lights, the room—but there’s no music in me. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everyone does, I guess—but this waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness… like everything’s there but there’s no music in me… that feels different. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in dancing or other things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I still care about dancing, and I don’t want to sound like I’ve just given up or anything. It’s just… some days it feels harder to feel the same spark. But I don’t know if that’s “lost interest,” exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a lot, anyway. I mean, I get worried here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It feels more like I’m just unsettled sometimes. Is that what you mean by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel pleasurable right now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I wouldn’t say things feel completely empty or anything. Maybe some things don’t land the same way right now, but I can’t tell if that’s just being tired or distracted. I still want to enjoy them, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, not in a terrible way, but I’ve noticed it. Like I’ll sit down to finish something straightforward, like a grant report, and then a truck outside or a photo on the wall or even one phrase in an old rehearsal note will pull me into some totally different idea or plan. I can usually come back to it eventually, but it’s been harder to stay on one thing. Is that what you mean by concentrating?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has gone down lately?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but when I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just forget to eat sometimes, like I’d look up and realize I’d skipped a meal. And now… food is there, but it kind of tastes like cardboard. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging a bit looser, so I guess my appetite has gone down. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you been waking up earlier than usual and not able to fall back asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every single night, but lately I’ll fall asleep because I’m just exhausted, and then I’m up around four or five with my stomach all tight, and I can’t really settle back down.\n\nBefore that there were a few nights where I stayed up way too late sketching formations, almost until dawn, but I told myself that was just work energy. I don’t know if that counts as sleep being “disturbed,” but it’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you were buzzing with ideas, did you feel unusually energized or high compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “unusually energized” is fair. I don’t want to overstate it, but there were stretches where I felt kind of dazzlingly alive, like every idea had light around it and I could see the whole piece coming together.\n\nAnd then, um… not as flattering, but I’d get really sharp with people if they questioned the plan or reminded me my ankle still needed rest. I’d feel furious suddenly, like they were trying to slow down something important. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did you feel unusually confident or powerful compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that way. At the time it didn’t feel like “powerful,” exactly. It felt more like I could turn the whole setback into something meaningful—like a career-defining piece, something that might genuinely change how people think about dance and wilderness.\n\nAnd I remember being kind of… shocked, honestly, when other people didn’t immediately see what I saw. I thought it was so obvious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, were you taking on a lot more projects or working much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than usual. I mean, I told myself it was just being productive, trying to make good use of being injured, but I was kind of bouncing from one thing to the next.\n\nI’d be drawing maps, then emailing collaborators, then reorganizing music files, then suddenly trying to redesign the whole project all at once. It felt important at the time, not chaotic exactly… but looking back, it was a lot. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did they cause serious problems in your life or work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I was still showing up for the essential things, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything completely fell apart.\n\nBut, yes, it did cause problems. I fell behind on my teaching prep, and I missed a grant deadline, which is… not like me. And the studio got so disorganized that collaborators started taking over basic scheduling things for me.\n\nI could still function, technically, but not at the level I normally do as a choreographer or teacher. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: About the time when you felt unusually energized and full of ideas, what’s the longest that stretch lasted?\n\nPatient: I guess… the longest stretch has really been this whole period since I hurt my ankle. It’s not like one single clear episode with a neat start and stop, but the pattern started after the injury and it’s been going on, in some form, for about two months now.\n\nI know that probably sounds like a long time. I kept thinking it was just me trying to stay useful while I couldn’t dance normally. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. A little, yes… maybe more than a little, if I’m being honest.

I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after something stressful happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it did. It was after I hurt my ankle. I mean, I know injuries happen, especially in dance, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s when this whole pattern seemed to start. It’s been about two months now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Does the sadness feel much stronger than you would expect from being injured?\n\nPatient: I mean… I keep telling myself it’s just an ankle, and people deal with worse, so maybe I shouldn’t make too much of it. But yes, it does feel… bigger than that, I think. I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness, like everything’s technically there—the lights, the room—but there’s no music in me. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everyone does, I guess—but this waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness… like everything’s there but there’s no music in me… that feels different. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in dancing or other things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I still care about dancing, and I don’t want to sound like I’ve just given up or anything. It’s just… some days it feels harder to feel the same spark. But I don’t know if that’s “lost interest,” exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a lot, anyway. I mean, I get worried here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It feels more like I’m just unsettled sometimes. Is that what you mean by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel pleasurable right now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I wouldn’t say things feel completely empty or anything. Maybe some things don’t land the same way right now, but I can’t tell if that’s just being tired or distracted. I still want to enjoy them, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, not in a terrible way, but I’ve noticed it. Like I’ll sit down to finish something straightforward, like a grant report, and then a truck outside or a photo on the wall or even one phrase in an old rehearsal note will pull me into some totally different idea or plan. I can usually come back to it eventually, but it’s been harder to stay on one thing. Is that what you mean by concentrating?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has gone down lately?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but when I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just forget to eat sometimes, like I’d look up and realize I’d skipped a meal. And now… food is there, but it kind of tastes like cardboard. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging a bit looser, so I guess my appetite has gone down. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you been waking up earlier than usual and not able to fall back asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every single night, but lately I’ll fall asleep because I’m just exhausted, and then I’m up around four or five with my stomach all tight, and I can’t really settle back down.\n\nBefore that there were a few nights where I stayed up way too late sketching formations, almost until dawn, but I told myself that was just work energy. I don’t know if that counts as sleep being “disturbed,” but it’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you were buzzing with ideas, did you feel unusually energized or high compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “unusually energized” is fair. I don’t want to overstate it, but there were stretches where I felt kind of dazzlingly alive, like every idea had light around it and I could see the whole piece coming together.\n\nAnd then, um… not as flattering, but I’d get really sharp with people if they questioned the plan or reminded me my ankle still needed rest. I’d feel furious suddenly, like they were trying to slow down something important. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did you feel unusually confident or powerful compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that way. At the time it didn’t feel like “powerful,” exactly. It felt more like I could turn the whole setback into something meaningful—like a career-defining piece, something that might genuinely change how people think about dance and wilderness.\n\nAnd I remember being kind of… shocked, honestly, when other people didn’t immediately see what I saw. I thought it was so obvious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, were you taking on a lot more projects or working much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than usual. I mean, I told myself it was just being productive, trying to make good use of being injured, but I was kind of bouncing from one thing to the next.\n\nI’d be drawing maps, then emailing collaborators, then reorganizing music files, then suddenly trying to redesign the whole project all at once. It felt important at the time, not chaotic exactly… but looking back, it was a lot. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did they cause serious problems in your life or work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I was still showing up for the essential things, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything completely fell apart.\n\nBut, yes, it did cause problems. I fell behind on my teaching prep, and I missed a grant deadline, which is… not like me. And the studio got so disorganized that collaborators started taking over basic scheduling things for me.\n\nI could still function, technically, but not at the level I normally do as a choreographer or teacher. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: About the time when you felt unusually energized and full of ideas, what’s the longest that stretch lasted?\n\nPatient: I guess… the longest stretch has really been this whole period since I hurt my ankle. It’s not like one single clear episode with a neat start and stop, but the pattern started after the injury and it’s been going on, in some form, for about two months now.\n\nI know that probably sounds like a long time. I kept thinking it was just me trying to stay useful while I couldn’t dance normally. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, were you talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… maybe more than a little, if I’m being honest.\n\nMy collaborators mentioned that in meetings I was talking really fast, like they couldn’t quite get a word in. I didn’t mean to steamroll anyone. It just felt like if I paused, the whole idea might disappear before I could explain it.\n\nI know that probably sounds intense. I thought I was just being excited about the work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes… I think so. I mean, I wasn’t doing more in a productive, organized way exactly, but I kept moving from one thing to the next.

Even with my ankle, I’d be sitting there drawing out maps for the piece, then emailing collaborators, then reorganizing music files, then suddenly trying to redesign the whole project all at once. It felt important at the time, like I was being useful.

I know that sounds like a lot. I wasn’t trying to be chaotic, but… yes, I was doing much more than usual.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, my sleep has just been kind of all over the place.

Some nights I’d tell myself I was just going to sketch a few formations, and then suddenly it would be almost dawn. And lately it’s more like I fall asleep because I’m completely exhausted, but then I wake up at four or five with my stomach all tight.

It’s been about two months now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Does the sadness feel much stronger than you would expect from being injured?\n\nPatient: I mean… I keep telling myself it’s just an ankle, and people deal with worse, so maybe I shouldn’t make too much of it. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had bad days before—everyone does, I guess—but this waking up with that heavy, hollow sadness… like everything’s there but there’s no music in me… that feels different. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in dancing or other things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I still care about dancing, and I don’t want to sound like I’ve just given up or anything. It’s just… some days it feels harder to feel the same spark. But I don’t know if that’s “lost interest,” exactly. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not a lot, anyway. I mean, I get worried here and there, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It feels more like I’m just unsettled sometimes. Is that what you mean by anxious?\n\nDoctor: Have there been things you usually enjoy that just don’t feel pleasurable right now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I wouldn’t say things feel completely empty or anything. Maybe some things don’t land the same way right now, but I can’t tell if that’s just being tired or distracted. I still want to enjoy them, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, not in a terrible way, but I’ve noticed it. Like I’ll sit down to finish something straightforward, like a grant report, and then a truck outside or a photo on the wall or even one phrase in an old rehearsal note will pull me into some totally different idea or plan. I can usually come back to it eventually, but it’s been harder to stay on one thing. Is that what you mean by concentrating?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has gone down lately?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but when I was really buzzing with ideas I’d just forget to eat sometimes, like I’d look up and realize I’d skipped a meal. And now… food is there, but it kind of tastes like cardboard. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging a bit looser, so I guess my appetite has gone down. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you been waking up earlier than usual and not able to fall back asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every single night, but lately I’ll fall asleep because I’m just exhausted, and then I’m up around four or five with my stomach all tight, and I can’t really settle back down.\n\nBefore that there were a few nights where I stayed up way too late sketching formations, almost until dawn, but I told myself that was just work energy. I don’t know if that counts as sleep being “disturbed,” but it’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you were buzzing with ideas, did you feel unusually energized or high compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think “unusually energized” is fair. I don’t want to overstate it, but there were stretches where I felt kind of dazzlingly alive, like every idea had light around it and I could see the whole piece coming together.\n\nAnd then, um… not as flattering, but I’d get really sharp with people if they questioned the plan or reminded me my ankle still needed rest. I’d feel furious suddenly, like they were trying to slow down something important. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did you feel unusually confident or powerful compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that way. I thought it was so obvious. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, were you taking on a lot more projects or working much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than usual. I mean, I told myself it was just being productive, trying to make good use of being injured, but I was kind of bouncing from one thing to the next.\n\nI’d be drawing maps, then emailing collaborators, then reorganizing music files, then suddenly trying to redesign the whole project all at once. It felt important at the time, not chaotic exactly… but looking back, it was a lot. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did they cause serious problems in your life or work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I was still showing up for the essential things, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything completely fell apart.\n\nBut, yes, it did cause problems. I fell behind on my teaching prep, and I missed a grant deadline, which is… not like me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: About the time when you felt unusually energized and full of ideas, what’s the longest that stretch lasted?\n\nPatient: I guess… the longest stretch has really been this whole period since I hurt my ankle. I kept thinking it was just me trying to stay useful while I couldn’t dance normally. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, were you talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… maybe more than a little, if I’m being honest.\n\nMy collaborators mentioned that in meetings I was talking really fast, like they couldn’t quite get a word in. I didn’t mean to steamroll anyone. It just felt like if I paused, the whole idea might disappear before I could explain it.\n\nI know that probably sounds intense. It felt important at the time, like I was being useful.\n\nI know that sounds like a lot. I wasn’t trying to be chaotic, but… yes, I was doing much more than usual.\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did you feel like you needed less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s been about two months now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Does the sadness feel much stronger than you would expect from being injured?\n\nPatient: I mean… I keep telling myself it’s just an ankle, and people deal with worse, so maybe I shouldn’t make too much of it. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I mean, not in a terrible way, but I’ve noticed it. Is that what you mean by concentrating?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has gone down lately?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you been waking up earlier than usual and not able to fall back asleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Um… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. It wasn’t like I was seeing people or anything.

Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, were you taking on a lot more projects or working much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… probably more than usual. I mean, I told myself it was just being productive, trying to make good use of being injured, but I was kind of bouncing from one thing to the next.\n\nI’d be drawing maps, then emailing collaborators, then reorganizing music files, then suddenly trying to redesign the whole project all at once. It felt important at the time, not chaotic exactly… but looking back, it was a lot. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did they cause serious problems in your life or work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I was still showing up for the essential things, so I don’t want to make it sound like everything completely fell apart.\n\nBut, yes, it did cause problems. I fell behind on my teaching prep, and I missed a grant deadline, which is… not like me. And the studio got so disorganized that collaborators started taking over basic scheduling things for me.\n\nI could still function, technically, but not at the level I normally do as a choreographer or teacher. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: About the time when you felt unusually energized and full of ideas, what’s the longest that stretch lasted?\n\nPatient: I guess… the longest stretch has really been this whole period since I hurt my ankle. It’s not like one single clear episode with a neat start and stop, but the pattern started after the injury and it’s been going on, in some form, for about two months now.\n\nI know that probably sounds like a long time. I kept thinking it was just me trying to stay useful while I couldn’t dance normally. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, were you talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… maybe more than a little, if I’m being honest.\n\nMy collaborators mentioned that in meetings I was talking really fast, like they couldn’t quite get a word in. I didn’t mean to steamroll anyone. It just felt like if I paused, the whole idea might disappear before I could explain it.\n\nI know that probably sounds intense. I thought I was just being excited about the work.\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, were you doing much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I wasn’t doing more in a productive, organized way exactly, but I kept moving from one thing to the next.\n\nEven with my ankle, I’d be sitting there drawing out maps for the piece, then emailing collaborators, then reorganizing music files, then suddenly trying to redesign the whole project all at once. It felt important at the time, like I was being useful.\n\nI know that sounds like a lot. I wasn’t trying to be chaotic, but… yes, I was doing much more than usual.\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did you feel like you needed less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, my sleep has just been kind of all over the place.\n\nSome nights I’d tell myself I was just going to sketch a few formations, and then suddenly it would be almost dawn. And lately it’s more like I fall asleep because I’m completely exhausted, but then I wake up at four or five with my stomach all tight.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as needing less sleep exactly, but it definitely hasn’t been normal for me.\n\nDoctor: During the time when you felt unusually energized and needed less sleep, did that elevated feeling last for at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: Um… yes, I think it was definitely more than four days.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but this whole pattern really started after my ankle injury, and it’s kind of been going on for about two months now. Not every single day was exactly the same, but the energized, not-sleeping-normal part… it wasn’t just a couple days. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During those energized times, did you ever hear or see things that other people could not?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t want to make it sound dramatic. It wasn’t like I was seeing people or anything.\n\nBut on a few of the really wired days, when I was alone in my apartment, I could hear this kind of low humming voice… almost like wind moving over rock. It felt like it was telling me where the dancers should go, like where they should move next.\n\nI know that probably sounds strange. The windows were closed, so… I don’t really know what to make of it."
